The 9 Best Binoculars, According to Our Tests and Research

📅 2019-10-18  |  Read Source →

See the world in greater detail with one of these recommended models.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. Why Trust Us?

Despite ubiquitous satellite imagery, incredible smartphone cameras, and a video doorbell on almost every house, there’s still incredible power in witnessing something with your own eyes. Unfortunately, despite eyesight being our dominant sense, our optics have some real limitations. Binoculars are considered relatively old technology, but they’re still recognized as the best tool to reach for when identifying a far-off animal or examining details on a backyard bird.

Hunters use binoculars to spot and pick out details on their quarry thousands of yards away, birders confirm species they’re identifying, and stargazers zoom in on constellations. They’re also just handy to keep in your house or even your vehicle for those moments when you just want to “see for yourself.”

You can extend your vision with telescopes, spotting scopes, and even zoom camera lenses, but the comfort and ease of use are why binoculars have such enduring appeal. Monocular devices—spotting scopes, for instance—can put strain on your eyes after only a few minutes, while binoculars offer a much more natural extension of normal vision, making them more comfortable for long sessions and easier for beginners to use. Binoculars are also lighter than those similar devices, which makes them packable and portable for trips into nature where they offer a more intimate look at shy creatures.

The challenge in shopping for a pair of binoculars is the sheer number and diversity of options from big, high-powered zooms that require a tripod to pocket-sized budget versions and everything in between. To help you narrow your search, we dive into how to select a pair that fits within your price range and how you intend to use them. We also share our favorites from years of testing in the field.

I’m a lifelong hunter and wildlife enthusiast who uses binoculars year-round for hunting as well as volunteer wildlife surveys for bighorn sheep and goats. Long hours behind glass, as well as more than a decade of testing dozens of pairs of binoculars in the field for editorial reviews, have informed my picks, along with conversations with brand representatives and optics dealers. For this update of my long-running guide, I tested six new pairs of binoculars that are new releases from established optics brands. Not all of them made the cut here, but several did, including the Nikon Trailblazer II 10x25, the first time I enjoyed a microcompact binocular enough to include it.

The new Leupold BX-3 Alpine HD binoculars deliver premium-enough outdoor optics while maintaining a price point almost everyone can stomach. The BX-2 Alpine HD, which occupied that exact niche for years, did it well enough that it became my favorite binocular in the midrange category and my everyday hunting optic.

This is a compact, lightweight binocular with good clarity and low-light performance, which makes it a solid choice for basically everyone from birders to stargazers, even though Leupold tends to market mostly to hunters and target shooters. While not the cheapest bino on the market, it’s worth the upgrade over the lowest-tier optics and, like any Leupold optic, comes with a lifetime warranty, meaning it could conceivably be the last pair you’ll ever buy.

The BX-3 doesn’t reinvent anything, but Leupold subtly refined the binos, updating coatings, tweaking ergonomics, and adding a few quality-of-life improvements while keeping the same general form factor, magnification options, and modest price point that made the BX-2 so successful in the first place.

The price jumped up slightly with the release of the BX-3, so while I’d buy the BX-3 for the improvements if you’re paying full price, retailers appear to be deeply discounting the existing stock of BX-2s, so it’s worth considering the older model if there’s a great price on it, at least until retailers run out of the now-discontinued model.

Read the Full Review of the Leupold BX-3 Here

Zeiss is synonymous with good glass in consumer camera lenses and sport optics, so the mid-tier-priced Terra ED 10x42 binoculars are somewhat of a surprise in the manufacturer’s lineup. I’ve tested several higher-end Zeiss binoculars, which were unsurprisingly great given their price tags, but finding nearly as good clarity and color in this $500 pair was unexpected.

While $500 isn’t cheap for most casual binocular buyers, I’d argue getting Zeiss quality at this price presents a serious value if you’re looking for good glass but aren’t ready to put down a mortgage payment to get it.

The 10x42 Terra ED uses ED glass and Zeiss's hydrophobic multicoating to produce a bright, high-contrast image that will feel like a serious upgrade to budget options. It's not going to match the edge-to-edge perfection or low-light performance of Zeiss's flagship binoculars, but it delivers enough optical quality to be an obvious jump in clarity for the investment.

The 10x42 configuration is a versatile middle ground for hunting, hiking, birding, and general wildlife observation. The Terra ED has a 330-foot field of view at 1,000 yards and a 5.3-foot close focus, so it is adequate for birding, casual stargazing, and wildlife watching. At 25.6 ounces, it's also light enough to carry comfortably in a chest harness (not included) for long stretches without pulling on your neck.

The fiberglass-reinforced housing is waterproof and nitrogen-filled to minimize fog and condensation outdoors. The hydrophobic coating helps keep moisture from becoming a major distraction when you're using the binoculars in wet weather or going between temperature extremes. The large focus wheel is also easy to find and operate quickly.

The Terra ED isn't the cheapest 10x42 on the market, but that's not really the point. It's a relatively affordable way into a serious optics brand, and the difference is visible when you use them side by side with cheaper binos.

Most hunters don't need the biggest or most expensive binoculars they can buy, but many with the budget and obsession with the sport will spend thousands hoping expensive optics can buy success. While $3,000 binoculars are overkill for most, hunters do ask a lot of their optics. Early morning and late evening light and long distances expose the weaknesses of lower-quality glass, so many hunters get the advice to buy the best glass they can afford.

While the top-tier binoculars are undoubtedly better, there’s a mid-tier sweet spot that gives you most of the performance of those four-figure binos at a fraction of the price. The Vortex Viper HD 10x42 binoculars are squarely there. Vortex’s Triumph binoculars are our Best Budget pick above for the value they provide at $100, but dedicated hunters who get up early and stay out late will eventually want to upgrade.

The Viper HD 10x42s have a list price around $729, but can be found at most retailers for closer to $500, a good value for the upgrade. Still, that’s $400 more than the Triumph, so you’ll want to be sure you value the improvements. The benefits over cheaper options are better light transmission and reduced distortions such as chromatic aberrations. If you’ve used cheaper binos, you’ve seen off colors such as purple fringes on trees. In broad daylight, this isn’t a problem you can’t adjust your eyes to, but in low light and longer distances it can result in straining and struggling to tell a stump from a bear or deer.

A relatively low 24.9-ounce weight and compact form factor enabled by the roof prism design make these comfortable to carry in a chest harness over long days in the field or a tree stand. The rubber housing offers satisfactory grip and I notably appreciated the underside thumb grooves, though they don’t look as high-end as magnesium housings. The included chest harness is capable enough, but will be a throwaway for hunters who already own a specialized hunting chest rig.

Turned off by cheap compact binoculars in the past? These are worth it. They offer quality optics with powerful-enough magnification for bird-watching, hunting, and a range of everyday uses. In addition, they’re sealed, so they're waterproof and fogproof, and there is a lifetime warranty on the optical parts so you won’t need to replace them if you drop these binos even once.

Nikon’s Trailblazer II 10x25 compact binoculars make some compromises on field of view and low-light performance, but on the plus side, they’re small enough to fit in your pocket. These are binoculars you can toss in a daypack for when you want them, rather than committing to positioning them on you, front and center, and dealing with the weight.

The trade-off, however, is the 25mm objective lens. There's simply less glass available to gather light than you'll get with a 42mm binocular, and the field of view isn’t bad but feels cramped if you’re used to bigger binos. The 10mm eye relief also isn't particularly generous for eyeglass wearers. But for quick looks during daylight, the compact size makes these much easier to have on you when you need them.

Bigger is typically better when it comes to optical performance in binoculars, but there is always a give-and-take. With standard 10x42 or larger binoculars, you get better performance, but usually at least a pound of weight and a bulky item. Bird-watchers and hunters use chest harnesses to distribute the weight and keep them accessible, but that’s a commitment you don’t always want for a casual hike.

The Trailblazer II has a more palatable price at $130 than you’ll find with larger binos with similar optical quality, making these a no-brainer if you’re shopping for casual use.

Like Leupold, Vortex has a great reputation, in large part due to its accommodating lifetime warranty program—a comfort to those of us who are hard on our gear. One challenge with Vortex is the sheer number of lines and models, but the newer Triumph line is easy to understand: it’s the brand’s cheapest pair of binoculars in the 10x42 build and arguably its best value at $100. While the optical clarity and quality aren’t on par with that of the more expensive lines, the Triumph does surprisingly well, especially in daylight.

Like most cheaper binos, the clarity falls off the closer you get to the edges of the field of view, but is sharp and color-true in the center. Yes, you can spend less, but I wouldn’t recommend it, having tried a lot of $25 to $50 binoculars over the years. Despite the attractive price, the Triumph ships with a case, harness, and neck strap as well.

Full Vortex Triumph 10x42 Binoculars Review

Nikon is best known for its cameras and lenses, but this Japanese optics brand makes a wide range of excellent binoculars as well. I’ve tested several models over the years, but the Monarch M7 stands out for the optical clarity it delivers at a mid-tier price point.

Nearly any pair of binoculars will work for birding, but for most species, you’ll be in trees and fairly close quarters where higher magnifications can make it harder to spot and track birds with the more zoomed-in view. Even when you spot a bird, even the slightly tighter 10x magnification can make it harder to follow flight.

The 8x42 version of the Monarch M7 binos gives you enough magnification to confirm details without spooking wildlife, but the wide field makes it easier to track them. The M5 series is also good and about $200 cheaper, but it has a narrow field of view even in the 8x42 configuration.

Like most binoculars, they’re nitrogen-purged and O-ring sealed, which just means they’re waterproof and fogproof. (Note: Fogproof only means there won’t be interior fog. You can still steam up the glass if, for example, your face is warm and the air or binos are cold.)

More money can, of course, get you even better glass from Nikon and other companies, but this is one of the best mid-tier options that delivers a clear and comfortable birding experience. Less money will downgrade your clarity, but cheaper and smaller options that weigh and cost much less abound if those are your priorities. (The entry-level Nocs Provisions Standard Issue 8x25 binos, for example, weigh about half as much.)

There are times when 10x binoculars simply aren't enough—and that’s especially true in Western hunting country where the animal you're looking for might be thousands of yards across a canyon. A 15x binocular like Maven's C.4 gives you considerably more reach while retaining the depth perception and comfort of looking through both eyes.

Extra reach is the reason to buy these. At 15x, details that can be frustratingly ambiguous through a conventional 10x42 become much easier to pick apart. I've found high-power binoculars particularly useful for searching large expanses of terrain where the ability to use both eyes makes extended glassing considerably more comfortable than squinting through a spotting scope. At distances of a mile or more, you’ll still want a spotter that’s 20x and up, but the 15x is a meaningful middle ground that’s wide enough to use in a forest, but reaches far enough for big mountain hunting.

The C.4 combines 15x magnification with large 56mm objective lenses, giving it a 215-foot field of view at 1,000 yards. This is noticeably tighter than, for example, the 342-foot field of view of the Leupold BX-3 10x42s, but that’s by design and the trade-off for 50 percent more zoom.

The other big trade-off is the weight and size. At 45 ounces, they’re still lighter than a spotting scope, but you’ll likely also be carrying a tripod, which adds several pounds to your pack. Compare this to 10x42s, which are usually 1 to 2 pounds total, and it’s a big difference. Still, if you’re using binoculars to cover big mountain distances and need the clarity and zoom to identify animals precisely, it’s likely a weight penalty you’ll be willing to pay.

The ED glass and phase-corrected, dielectric-coated prisms produce a surprisingly sharp image, and the 92 percent light transmission is key around dawn and dusk—impressive for the price. Most high-quality, high-power binoculars are at least four figures, but Maven delivers the C.4 for $550. Unlike the rest of its line that uses Japanese and Chinese components that are assembled in Wyoming, the C.4 is produced entirely in China. Despite this difference, I didn’t notice a sharp drop-off in build or optical quality from other Maven binos I’ve tested.

There's a catch: These aren't really handheld binoculars. At 45.1 ounces, the C.4 is almost twice the weight of many conventional 10x42s, and 15x magnification makes even small hand movements obvious. I used the C.4 handheld fine for short periods, but the size and weight wear on your stability much faster than lightweight hunting binos. Once mounted, however, the extra weight only affects your pack and the C.4 becomes a remarkably capable long-distance optic.

Any decent pair of binoculars can enhance casual stargazing, but if you’re closer to “astrophile” than “occasional noticer of the night sky,” you’ll want a somewhat specialized pair and the best low-light performance you can afford. As binoculars go up in price, into the thousands, the returns are diminishing, giving you small increments of improved clarity and edge-case benefits, but they are better.

Celestron is a budget binocular brand, but its SkyMaster series is astro-focused and worth a look if you’re focused on stars. The massive objective lenses, high magnification, and Porro prism combine for a heavy build, but one that’s designed for night use at the distance of light-years.

The 25x magnification won’t be much good for backyard bird-watching or any handheld use, but they’re designed for tripod mounting, and for use as a stationary sky-watching setup that’s more comfortable for longer sessions than telescopes.

Going with binoculars for sky watching, especially ones as zoomed-in as these, adds a layer of dimensionality that’s lost looking with the naked eye (or with one eye as with telescopes). Unlike cheap Amazon optics brands, Celestron has friendly price points but still gives you a lifetime warranty. If your stargazing habit sticks, you may eventually want to upgrade to a higher-end model, but this pair will show you what you’ve been missing for a few hundred bucks.

Any decent pair of binoculars can enhance casual stargazing, but if you’re closer to “astrophile” than “occasional noticer of the night sky,” you’ll want a somewhat specialized pair and the best low-light performance you can afford. As binoculars go up in price, into the thousands, the returns are diminishing, giving you small increments of improved clarity and edge-case benefits, but they are better.

If your stargazing habit sticks, you may eventually want to upgrade to a higher-end model, but this pair will show you what you’ve been missing for a few hundred bucks.

Yes, this is an article about binoculars, but I humbly present the little-known monocular as a better tool for certain jobs. Like spotting scopes, monoculars aren’t as natural to use since you have to close one eye and miss out on the depth of a stereo picture. However, there are times when the weight and bulk of binos mean you leave them at home even though you might want them in the field.

I personally carry this Nocs Field Tube monocular every time I go backcountry skiing and find it quite literally worth its weight for scouting potential approach and descent lines, using it to pick out details about snow quality, avalanches, and terrain that my naked eye regularly misses. It’s also easy to throw in a pack or pocket for casual hikes or pre-hunting season scouting walks where I never know when I might want a closer look at something but am not motivated enough to harness up with my binoculars.

There aren’t a lot of companies making monoculars, but Nocs has several options. I love the 8x32 size of the Field Tube, but you can get a 10x32 version that offers a bit more zoom but a slightly narrower field of view in the same size package. There’s also the newer 8x32 Zoom Tube that’s significantly cheaper and slightly lighter and smaller but isn’t fully waterproof.

Not everything we test or like ends up in our buying guide, but some are products that still warrant a mention. Here are a few.

Vortex Crossfire HD 10x42: These budget binos fall between the Triumph and Viper models we recommend above. They’re solid optics for the cost, but in a no-man’s-land for the price. If you’re on a tight budget, the Triumph 10x42s are less than half the price and nearly as good. And if you’re looking to upgrade to the mid-tier, it’s worth saving up for the Vipers or the Zeiss Terra ED 10x42s for a more noticeable jump in quality in a compact all-arounder.Nocs Field Issue 10x42: Give us some fun colors and a grippy, overmolded texture like on these 10x42s. But similar to the Vortex Crossfire, they’re not enough of an improvement on our best budget pick to justify double the cost. If you like the design that stands out from a crowd of bland black options, you won’t be disappointed, but we prefer to pay for performance.Zeiss SFL 12x50: These are awesome high-end binoculars that pack a little extra zoom as well as a large, 50mm objective lens that pulls in extra light for early and late. They also are incredibly compact and light despite their specs. The problem is the $2,000 price tag that puts them out of reach of most casual users. They’re really good, just not 10 times as good as a solid $200 pair from our list above.

The magnifications of most consumer binoculars are fixed (i.e., not zoomable), making it one of the most important decisions for buyers. More isn't always better and can feel claustrophobic in tight quarters such as trying to track a small bird in dense forest, while wider-angle, lower-power lenses can feel underpowered if you're trying to see across longer distances.

Most binos range between 7x and 18x. Much less than 7x isn’t enough of an effect to bother with for most applications, while 15x and up becomes difficult to use without a tripod. In general, lower-power binoculars are a better fit if you’re mostly glassing shorter distances, such as in forests, and don’t need precise detail. Higher-power binoculars make sense if you’re looking to avoid also carrying a spotting scope and glassing longer distances up to a mile or need precise detail for identifying wildlife.

Objective lens diameter is the size of the lens farthest from your eyes and impacts the field of vision you get, as well as how bright the image is since, generally, bigger lenses capture more light. Jeremy Bentham, senior manager of SRO Sales & Operations for Nikon, explains the trade-offs associated with objective lens size.

“One of the key things that needs to be decided is brightness versus weight,” he says. “Larger objective lenses allow more light into the optic but are also heavier to pack and carry with you.”

Objective lens sizes in binoculars range anywhere from 20 mm to 100 mm, but the common sizes are mostly in the 30 mm to 60 mm range. I find ultracompact binoculars of less than about 30 mm annoying to use for more than a quick look at something nearby since the field of view is small and it can be harder to keep in position on your eyes. On the other end of the spectrum, most binoculars larger than about 55 mm become heavy and unwieldy if used without a tripod.

Binoculars are listed by size spec, where the first number is magnification and the second is objective lens diameter. Probably the most common size is 10x42, which permits a big enough field of view and magnification for viewing things as near as 15 feet but still allows you to spot larger objects such as wildlife as far as a mile away.

For backcountry hunters and hikers, weight is one of the most important considerations in a binocular. You need to balance the desire for a larger, brighter optic with your ability and interest to carry it around your neck over long distances. For me, anything heavier than about 35 ounces is too much to carry in a chest harness when actively hunting and covering several miles a day. If you’re a hunter or hiker who doesn’t need ready access to your binoculars and can keep them in a pack until you need them at specific spots, you may be able to tolerate heavier options.

Even folks who mostly use binoculars at home or from a vehicle need to consider weight. Heavier binoculars are harder to use for longer periods of time since your arms and shoulders do eventually fatigue and become shaky. If you’re mostly using binoculars on a tripod (a must for higher-magnification binos), you may want to prize optical quality over weight savings.

Aside from range-finding binoculars, most have surprisingly little tech as we usually think of it in terms of electronics and connectivity. What makes great binoculars hasn’t changed much over the years and still boils down to quality glass and craftsmanship.

Where tech does show up is in the glass quality and the coatings used. High-tech coatings are used to make colors true, enhance brightness, and protect your expensive lenses. Coatings and glass often use nonstandardized terms such as ED, HD, and UHD glass, or proprietary branding such as LotuTec or Swarobright, making it hard to compare apples to apples when talking about something as subjective as optical quality. What you can look for is whether lenses are “fully multi-coated,” meaning that all glass surfaces have multiple coatings for durability and performance, whatever the branding.

Jamie, Senior Reviews Editor, joined the Hearst Enthusiast Group in 2021. She has covered technology and consumer lifestyle gadgets since 1995—and shared her expertise in print, digital, and in broadcast originating the role of The Gizmo Girl for ESPN’s Cold Pizza. She has written about, tested, and reviewed everything from turkey fryers to high-definition TVs. Her byline has appeared in TWICE, Sound & Vision, Consumer Reports, and many others. In her free time, Jamie is in a yoga class, searching for the perfect matcha latte, or walking the boards.

Spiced and Glazed Roast Turkey

📅 2019-10-15  |  Read Source →

Our mission: A roast turkey that’s boldly spiced, beautifully glazed, and—can we type this loudly enough?—PROPERLY COOKED. That means juicy breast meat, tender dark meat, and plenty of deeply browned skin. Andy Baraghani and Brad Leone’s solution? Break the bird into five pieces: two wings, two legs, and one whole breast. Roasting the bird in parts gives you more control over when each piece is done—no dry breast meat or tough legs.

They didn’t slouch on flavor either. A peppery dry brine with garlic powder, onion powder, and smoked paprika gets to work 24–48 hours before the turkey goes into the oven. Then comes the glaze: a savory-tangy mix of soy sauce, vinegar, brown sugar, orange zest, and herbs. Brushed onto the turkey as it roasts, it builds a glossy, deeply burnished finish. And because the pieces cook on a wire rack set inside a baking sheet, you get more exposed skin for browning and a more even cook overall.

If the idea of doing surgery on a raw turkey gives you a stress rash, ask your butcher to do it—even at the supermarket. Request a 12–14-lb. turkey cut into five pieces, with the thighs attached to the drumsticks, the breast left whole, and the backbone removed. If they ask why, just say: Yes, you’re making that ridiculous Bon Appétit recipe. If you’d rather do the honors yourself, here’s our guide to breaking down a whole turkey.

Choose an unbrined turkey for this recipe. Many supermarket birds are already treated with a salt solution, sometimes labeled “self-basting,” “enhanced,” or “contains a solution.” Adding this dry brine on top can make the turkey overly salty. Check the packaging before you buy.

Should I rinse off the dry brine before roasting?

No. Leave the seasoning on the turkey when you coat it with oil. Some of the spice mixture may rub off, but that’s fine. There’s no need to rinse the bird, and washing raw poultry can spread bacteria around your kitchen.

Can I use the pan drippings to make gravy?

Yes, but taste them first. Between the seasoned dry brine and soy-based glaze, the drippings can be quite salty and intensely flavored. Use them sparingly and taste your gravy before adding more salt. For a simpler option, try our Make-Ahead Gravy, which doesn’t require drippings at all.

Read our guide to turkey cooking times, no matter the bird’s size or your preferred cooking method.

4 hours 45 minutes, plus at least 24 hours dry-brining

Place one 12–14-lb. turkey, neck and giblets removed, breast side up, on a cutting board and pat dry. Grip a wing and pull it outward so you can see where it attaches to the body. Using a sharp boning or chef’s knife, cut through the joint to separate the wing from the breast (if you hit bone, you’re in the wrong spot; pull the wing out farther to help you get into the place where the joint meets the socket). Remove wing; repeat on the other side.

Cut through skin connecting 1 leg to carcass. Pull leg back until the ball joint pops out of its socket; cut through the joint to separate leg. Repeat on the other side.

Now for the breast: Position the turkey breast side down with the opening facing you. Using kitchen shears, cut along one side of backbone until you get all the way through the neck end, then turn the turkey 180° and cut through the other side to remove the backbone (save it for making stock).

Very finely grind 2 Tbsp. whole black peppercorns and 2 Tbsp. whole pink or black peppercorns in a spice mill or mortar and pestle; transfer to a medium bowl. Add ½ cup Diamond Crystal or ¼ cup plus 1½ tsp. Morton kosher salt, 2 Tbsp. garlic powder, 2 Tbsp. onion powder, 1 Tbsp. light brown sugar, and 1 Tbsp. smoked paprika, and use your fingers to mix together.

Place turkey pieces, skin side up, on a wire rack set inside a rimmed baking sheet. Sprinkle salt mixture liberally all over turkey, patting to adhere. You may not need all of it, but it’s good to have extra since some will end up on the baking sheet. Chill bird, uncovered, at least 24 hours and up to 2 days.

Remove turkey from wire rack and rinse baking sheet and rack if needed (turkey will most likely release some liquid). Line baking sheet with 3 layers of foil to make cleanup easy and set rack back inside. Arrange turkey pieces on rack, skin side up, and let sit at room temperature up to 2 hours to help decrease the cooking time.

Meanwhile, cook small handful of hardy herbs (such as sage, rosemary, bay leaves, and/or thyme), 4 garlic cloves, crushed, two 2x1" strips orange zest, ⅓ cup low-sodium soy sauce, ⅓ cup (packed; 71 g) light brown sugar, and ⅓ cup sherry vinegar or red wine vinegar in a small saucepan over medium heat, stirring occasionally, until sugar is dissolved and glaze thickens slightly (it should just barely coat a spoon), 10–12 minutes. Remove glaze from heat.

Place a rack in middle of oven; preheat to 425°. Rub turkey with 6 Tbsp. neutral oil (such as vegetable, peanut, or canola) and pour 1 cup water into baking sheet. Roast turkey, rotating baking sheet halfway through, until skin is mostly golden brown, 20–25 minutes. Reduce oven temperature to 300° and continue to roast turkey, brushing with glaze every 20 minutes and adding more water by ½-cupfuls as needed to maintain some liquid in baking sheet, until an instant-read thermometer inserted into the thickest part of breast registers 150°, and 170° when inserted into the thickest part of the thighs, 50–70 minutes longer (total cooking time will be 1–1½ hours). Skin should be deep golden brown and shiny. Transfer turkey to a cutting board; tent loosely with foil. Let rest 30–60 minutes before carving.

Editor’s note: This recipe was originally published in our November 2019 issue. We’ve updated the introduction with additional cooking guidance and answers to common reader questions; we’ve also updated the recipe for style. Head this way for more of our favorite Thanksgiving recipes →

Turkey Pot Pie

📅 2019-10-02  |  Read Source →

Chicken pot pie is a perfect cozy dinner for chilly fall days, but like most classics, it could use an upgrade. This turkey pot pie is a perfect way to switch up your meal rotation and make sure those Thanksgiving turkey leftovers don’t go to waste. That being said, this dinner is so comforting, it deserves to be made all season long.

What People Are Saying:“This was AMAZING. The filling is delicious, the crust is super flaky and golden brown. I will make this again for sure.” - GoldTeepee

Pop your butter in the freezer for about 15 minutes before you start making the crust so it’s adequately cold. Grab a large food processor and pulse together the flour, sugar, and salt until they’re mixed. Toss in the butter and pulse again until you see pea-sized pieces and larger chunks.

With the motor running, add vinegar and four tablespoons of ice water, one tablespoon at a time, until the dough just comes together and becomes moist. Make sure the dough isn’t wet and sticky or too crumbly (you can test this by squeezing some with your fingers). If the dough is too crumbly and not coming together, add more ice water, 1 teaspoon at a time.

Turn the dough out onto a lightly floured surface and gently squeeze it together. Knead it just a couple of times so it comes together, then split it into two balls. Flatten each one into a smooth disk, wrap them up in plastic, and pop them in the fridge for at least an hour or even overnight if you want to get ahead.

Move your oven rack to the center, then preheat to 375°. Grab a big, deep skillet and melt the butter over medium heat. Toss in the onions and carrots, and stir until they’re soft. Give it about 7 to 8 minutes. Next, add the garlic and cook, stirring, just until it smells amazing (about a minute). Now, grab a spatula or wooden spoon and stir in the flour. Cook, scraping up any tasty bits from the bottom, until the mixture turns golden and starts to bubble—just 2 or 3 minutes.

Pour the wine in slowly, stirring as you go. Let it simmer for about 3 minutes, just until that alcohol smell is gone. Then, slowly add 1 1/3 cups of cream, still scraping up all the good stuff from the bottom. Keep cooking until the mixture starts to boil and thickens up a bit. This should take around 5 minutes.

Fold in the turkey, peas, thyme, and parsley. Give it all a good season with kosher salt and pepper. Take the skillet off the heat and let the filling cool until you’re ready to use it.

Butter up a 9" pie dish or a deep skillet for your crust. On a lightly floured surface, roll out one disk of dough into a circle about 16" across and 1/4" thick. Carefully lay the dough into the pie dish, letting the edges slump down naturally. Trim the dough, leaving roughly a 1" overhang. Finally, pour in the cooled filling.

Roll out the second disk of dough into a 16"-diameter round about 1/4" thick and arrange it on top of the filling. Fold the top overhang into the bottom, pressing firmly to seal. Crimp the edges if you’d like. Using a paring knife, cut slits on top, and brush with the remaining 2 tablespoons of cream and sprinkle with sea salt.

Bake the pie (cover the edges with foil if they start getting too brown) until the crust is golden and the filling is bubbling. This takes about 1 hour 15 minutes. Once it’s done, move the pie to a wire rack and let it cool for about 15 minutes before digging in.

The full list of ingredients and instructions can be found in the recipe below.

Cover your pot pie tightly with plastic wrap or store in an airtight container in the fridge for up to five days after baking. If you’d like to make your pot pie ahead of time and freeze it, wrap the whole (baked or unbaked) turkey pot pie tightly in plastic wrap and store in the freezer for up to 2 months.

Turkey Cheese Ball

📅 2019-09-30  |  Read Source →

The turkey may be the main event of Thanksgiving dinner, but it can be a snack, too! Whether you're hosting Thanksgiving for your community or celebrating with family, this fun appetizer is the perfect way to get everyone excited for the big turkey dinner with another smaller turkey! This cheese ball is not only the most eye-catching appetizer you can add to your holiday menu, but it’s surprisingly easy to create.

What People Are Saying:“I made this for Friendsgiving. It was delicious and I got several comments on how great it tasted.” - kellykcox34580

In a food processor, pulse the cream cheese, goat cheese, butter, and garlic powder until the mixture is smooth. Add parsley, chives, and thyme and pulse until well combined. Then season with salt and black pepper.

Transfer the cream cheese mixture to a piece of plastic wrap and gather up the wrap to form a ball. Then transfer the mixture into a large bowl and refrigerate until it’s firm, which should take at least three hours and up to one day.

In a clean food processor, pulse 3/4 cup pecan halves until they are finely chopped.

Roll the cheese ball in nuts and arrange it all on a platter.

Start by cutting a large triangle from a red bell pepper—this will be the face. Next, cut a smaller triangle from a yellow bell pepper for the beak. For the eyes, slice two small rounds from a small carrot. Take a larger carrot and slice it into diagonal ovals to make the feet, then carve little triangles at one end to look like toes. Finally, thinly slice the rest of the carrots into matchsticks for the tail.

Grab some toothpicks and stick the carrot eyes onto the red triangle face. Then, use another toothpick to add the yellow beak to the face—just make sure all the toothpicks point toward the back so you can attach everything to the cheese ball later. Now, press the face onto the front of the cheese ball, and slide the carrot feet into the base.

Insert matchstick carrots, pretzel sticks, and mini pretzels into the back of the ball to create the tail. Using the remaining 1/2 cup pecan halves, create wings by overlapping them along each side of the ball. Serve with crackers alongside!

The full list of ingredients and instructions can be found in the recipe below.

Store leftover cheese ball in an airtight container in the refrigerator for up to 4 days. If possible, remove the pretzels and vegetable decorations before storing, as they can soften over time.

Taylor Swift and Karlie Kloss’s Complete Friendship Timeline

📅 2019-09-24  |  Read Source →

Taylor Swift has long documented the peaks and valleys of her relationships in precise pop detail. This fact makes her rumored rift with former best friend Karlie Kloss all the more intriguing. Once the closest of pals (a joint Vogue cover!), the two seemingly grew estranged, though their relationship has since shown signs of healing.

Below, a history of Karlie and Taylor: The Way They Were.

Kloss read the piece and tweeted, “Hey @taylorswift13 love the @voguemagazine cover! Your kitchen or mine? :)”

A few months later, Swift and Kloss opted for a bestie road trip. Kloss documented it on her Instagram. "karlie ❤️'s @taylorswift," Kloss captioned a photo of her tracing that very same message into a sandy beach. “another epic pit stop...Do you see the size of that elephant seal?!” Kloss wrote alongside a snap of the two hugging during their travels.

The pair was also seen together at a pre-Oscar party, courtesy of Gigi Hadid’s Twitter.

During the spring, the two friends were spotted out and about in New York City. Their strolls through SoHo solidified the growing friendship—and the fact that they might have been two of the tallest celebrity BFFs ever.

In May, the friends attended the Met Gala. “Pre #MetGala glam party with @taylorswift,” Kloss wrote next to a selfie of the pals doing their makeup together.

Kloss and Swift’s summer in the city showed no signs of stopping. They ran errands in SoHo and grabbed lunch at Sarabeth’s in Tribeca.

For her Rolling Stone cover story, Swift gave the reporter a tour of her home, including the guest room “where Karlie usually stays.” Photos of the supermodel reportedly graced the wall, while a basket of her favorite Whole Foods snacks resided bedside.

Kloss and Swift were next spotted enjoying beers at a Knicks game.

Swift and Kloss were seen out and about in NYC again. Plus, the singer took Kloss as one of her dates to the American Music Awards, where she performed “Blank Space.”

Swift performed at another Victoria’s Secret Fashion Show, singing hits from her album 1989 as Kloss strutted by in lingerie and wings. Kloss shared a photo of the two walking backstage with the caption, “Cause we never go out of style, We never go out of style ❤️👯❤️.”

Months later, Swift and Kloss enjoyed a friendship milestone that few ever reach: appearing on the cover of Vogue together. Amidst stories told about their friendship, Swift said, “People had been telling us for years we needed to meet. I remember makeup artists and hair people going, 'Doesn’t she remind you of Karlie? God, she and Karlie would be best friends. They’re the same.’”

Kloss appeared in Swift’s celeb-filled music video for “Bad Blood,” an ode to her now-infamous squad. Other cameos came from pals including Selena Gomez, Zendaya, Lena Dunham, and Cara Delevingne. The song was considered to be about Swift’s feud with Katy Perry.

Kloss dropped by a London tour date of Swift’s 1989 tour. She attended the show with a group of other Swift squad members including Hadid, Delevingne, Serena Williams, and Kendall Jenner.

Swift also brought members of her girl gang to the MTV VMAs, which included Kloss, Gomez, Hadid, and Mariska Hargitay.

"Coachella ✅," Kloss captioned a selfie of herself, a bleach blond Swift, Todrick Hall, and Danielle Haim at the music festival.

“Made in the USA™,” Kloss wrote alongside a photo of herself and Swift at one of the musician’s legendary Rhode Island Fourth of July parties. (This was also the summer of the Hiddleswift romance seen ’round the world.)

In August, mere weeks after Swift’s feud with Kim Kardashian and Kanye West reared its snakelike head, Kloss was asked about the conflict. Sunday Times Style magazine directly asked Kloss if she thought Kardashian “was a good person.”

Kloss replied, “Ha... you know... I honestly... I... I...” She stuttered initially with a smile. Then she said, “I think she’s been a lovely person to me in the past. Look, I really don’t know her that well.”

Kloss later tweeted her statement on the subject publicly. “I will not allow the media to misconstrue my words,” the model wrote. “Taylor has always had my back and I will always have hers.”

In one of the pair’s last public sightings together for quite some time, Swift and Kloss were seen exiting the Bowery Ballroom alongside Serena Williams and Dakota Johnson.

“Happiest of Birthdays to my ride or die @taylorswift ❤️🎂👯❤️ I feel blessed to count you as my friend, sister and partner in crime. Can’t wait to celebrate together very soon ❤️🍪🍾,” Kloss wrote in a sweet post for Swift’s 27th birthday. Days later, Kloss would be photographed entering Swift’s Tribeca apartment for a party that also included Justin Timberlake, Jay-Z, and Beyoncé.

A year later, Kloss posted for Swift’s birthday, notably with far less to say about the day. “Happy happy birthday @taylorswift! 💫” she wrote.

After months of speculation over Kloss and Swift’s friendship (including the model’s lack of public support for Swift’s new single “Look What You Made Me Do”), fans detected shade. On Instagram, Kloss captioned a video of her playing basketball for Love magazine, “Swish swish 🏀❤️ Love Advent ’17 thank you @thelovemagazine @kegrand @philpoynter.” Fans noticed Kloss seemingly referenced Perry’s rumored Swift diss track, “Swish, Swish.” Soon after, Kloss changed the caption to read, “Nothing but net 🏀❤️.”

Rumors of a rift further circulated when People reported Kloss and Perry had grabbed dinner in Los Angeles with Vanity Fair writer Derek Blasberg and jewelry designer Jennifer Meyer.

The possible friendship breakup was even weighing heavily on Oscar winner Jennifer Lawrence. During a TimesTalks Q&A for her spy movie Red Sparrow, Lawrence said that if she were a spy in real life, she’d “like to know what’s going on with Karlie Kloss and Taylor Swift, that’s the honest to God truth.” She jokingly added that “what happened” between them is “keeping me up at night.”

The New York Times ran a profile of Kloss in which it listed Swift as one of Kloss’s “closest friends.” The piece also concluded that “despite internet gossip suggesting otherwise, the pair continues to be good friends and they talk frequently.” The model warned, “Don’t believe everything you read.”

Kloss momentarily put rumors to rest by attending the Nashville stop of Swift’s Reputation tour. “No one puts on a show like @taylorswift 🎤✨ #ReputationTour Nashville was out of this world, I am SO proud of you 🖤,” she captioned a selfie of the two.

During a “73 Questions” segment with Vogue, the interviewer asked Kloss about the friendship, saying, “I just have to get this out of the way, because the world deserves to know: Is everything cool with you and Taylor?”

Kloss laughed, “The world needs to know? Well, Jennifer Lawrence was interested. Jen, don’t worry, Taylor and I are still really good friends.”

After months spent apart, Andy Cohen asked Kloss her thoughts on Swift’s “30 Things I Learned Before Turning 30” essay for ELLE during Watch What Happens Live. In the essay, Swift wrote about learning the difference between lifelong friendships and situationships and leaving some friendships behind.

“Taylor is incredible, and I feel really lucky to call her a friend, and she’s one of the hardest-working women, and I loved her essay,” Kloss responded.

While many fans had already assumed that Swift and Kloss were not the partners in crime they once were, two developments in 2019 seemed to provide the final word on their relationship: Swift did not attend either of Kloss’s weddings to Joshua Kushner. The first was an 80-person ceremony in October 2018, which conflicted with Swift’s Reputation tour in Australia. June 2019 brought the second ceremony, attended by Swift enemies past and present, Katy Perry and Scooter Braun. Swift was invited to both, but Kloss’s rep said she was “unable to attend due to prior commitments.”

But perhaps the bigger blow to the friendship between Kloss and Swift came when the model was seen on Instagram with her manager, Scooter Braun. Braun had recently acquired all of the rights to Swift’s first six albums, much to the singer’s public dismay.

With the surprise release of Swift’s eighth album folklore on July 23, 2020, fans found any reason to tie Kloss’s social media activity to the music. After Swift teased her album with a woodsy photo on Instagram, Kloss shared two photos of herself in nature, captioned, “happy place 😌.” Some interpreted this as a sly nod to folklore’s aesthetic.

That same day, Klossy posted about her coding program, Kode With Klossy. In one of the videos, the code reads, “Easter egg,” which some Swifties took as reference to the singer’s own reliance on covert clues.

While those theories might have been a stretch, Kloss supported folklore on the ’gram in other ways. E! reported that Kloss liked photos of model Martha Hunt wearing a cardigan from Swift captioned with “peter losing wendy,” a lyric from the song “cardigan.” She also double-tapped Project Runway co-star Christian Siriano’s Swift-inspired artwork. “Well #taylorswift inspired a paint session last night after a very hard day in fashion,” he wrote alongside snaps of his painting. “So her wonderstruck gown painting is now available on ChristianSiriano.com! Oh and my custom Siriano little sofa is fitting in just perfectly!”

Another Swift album release meant more speculation about Swift’s friendship with Kloss. After Swift debuted two bonus tracks from her ninth album, evermore, rumors swirled about the inspiration behind both. In the song, “it's time to go,” Swift sings, “When the words of a sister, come back in whispers, that prove she was not, in fact, what she seemed / Not a twin from your dreams, she’s a crook who was caught.” That line seems to reference a falling out with a friend and many have assumed it’s Kloss.

Kloss has referred to Swift as being like her “sister” in the past, and numerous articles have called the two out for twinning.

The other new track, “right where you left me,” could also feature a Kloss callout. “Friends break up, friends get married,” is the lyric that opens the song, a possible reference to Swift not attending either of Kloss’s 2019 wedding ceremonies to Kushner.

Alas, Swift hasn’t commented on exactly who influenced either new song. Instead, she tweeted, “The first (‘right where you left me’) is a song about a girl who stayed forever in the exact spot where her heart was broken, completely frozen in time. ‘it’s time to go’ is about listening to your gut when it tells you to leave. How you always know before you know, you know?”

Kloss made headlines when she was spotted in the audience for Taylor Swift’s final Los Angeles Eras Tour show. Unlike other celeb guests, Kloss was not in the VIP tent but rather seated in the general population stands. Kloss did not post about the concert on her social media immediately after.

After Swift and Travis Kelce announced their engagement on Instagram, Kloss subtly acknowledged the post by liking it. The gesture marked her show of support, joining a number of other celebrities who reacted to the couple’s news, including Taylor Lautner and Sabrina Carpenter.

Kloss was photographed in New York with Kushner, both of them seemingly on their way to Madison Square Garden to attend Swift and Kelce’s wedding celebrations.

On July 28, Kloss spoke briefly about attending Swift’s wedding while on Today. “Listen, I try to protect the privacy of my friends’ personal lives,” Kloss prefaced. “But what I will say is that they’re an amazing couple, and I’m so happy for them.”

On November 8, Swift and Kloss were photographed together watching Kelce and the Kansas City Chiefs take on the New York Jets at Arrowhead Stadium. Kloss joined Swift in a suite alongside the singer’s parents, Andrea and Scott Swift.

Robert Pattinson and Suki Waterhouse’s Complete Relationship Timeline

📅 2019-08-23  |  Read Source →

Despite Robert Pattinson’s big-screen career and Suki Waterhouse’s supermodel status, the public doesn’t know much about their relationship. Both of these Brits prefer to keep their private lives out of the public sphere. As their romance continues past the five-year mark and the couple settles into parenthood, here’s a rundown of their secretive story.

Pattinson and Waterhouse were first linked on July 30 when E! obtained photos of the pair kissing in the streets of London. Per the outlet’s report, the two saw a 9 P.M. showing of Mamma Mia! Here We Go Again at Electric Cinema in Notting Hill. They then walked across the street for drinks at SoHo House until 1 A.M.An eyewitness said the couple was open with their affection. “Along the way, they were very close and cuddled and kissed many times,” the source told E! News. “Robert was very affectionate to her and had his arm around her the whole way and Suki reciprocated by holding his hand and kissing it. They were very loved up and close. Robert stroked Suki’s hair and gave her another loving kiss.”

An E! source said the pair was only “casually dating.” Since Pattinson had only recently ended a three-year relationship, “he isn’t looking for anything serious right now.”

The insider did express that the two “are definitely into each other” and are dating while Pattinson is in London filming The King. “Rob likes to have fun when he has breaks in-between filming and him and Suki have a great time together,” the source added.

The Sunday Times asked Pattinson about Waterhouse, and he responded by asking, “Do I have to [talk about her]?” Then he went into more detail about why he’s so private about his personal life:

“If you let people in, it devalues what love is. If a stranger on the street asked you about your relationship, you’d think it extremely rude. If you put up a wall it ends up better. I can’t understand how someone can walk down the street holding hands, and it’s the same as when I do it and a hundred people are taking your photo. The line between when you’re performing and when you’re not will eventually get washed away, and you’ll go completely mad.”

However, he did tell the outlet that breakups with Kristen Stewart and FKA Twigs didn’t disillusion him to romance with a celebrity. “They were pretty long relationships, not like three months.”

But then I was thinking that’s very wrong, a sad bear walking in circles. I just can’t help it. I’ll do it for every single element, every decision, in my life. What is the worst-case scenario for this decision?”

And I’m sitting there facing the other direction, and my girlfriend just keeps continuing the conversation with him. The 9 Best Running Shoes for Runners With Flat Feet and Low Arches

📅 2019-01-08  |  Read Source →

No, you don’t need more arch support. But here’s what you do need.

We earn a commission for products purchased through some links in this article. Why Trust Us?

Editor’s Note, November 6, 2026: Our Best Overall, the Brooks Adrenaline GTS 25, is 19% off! Our team is currently testing the Adrenaline GTS 26, which is on sale now.

Shopping for running shoes can be especially frustrating if you have flat feet. There are conflicting opinions about what types of shoes best accommodate your foot shape. Some shoe brands add lots of arch support. Other experts say the opposite. What’s a flat-footed runner to do?

Despite the confusion, there are solutions out there, especially with flat feet being more common than you think. Finding shoes that work with your foot type, support good running form, and feel comfortable is paramount—and totally doable.

Not all flat feet are the same, which means there’s no universal solution that will work well for everyone. We’ll go over what it means to have flat feet and some of the potential issues the condition can present for runners. We’ve also highlighted shoe designs that might help alleviate any pain or discomfort you’re experiencing. Finally, we’ve picked the best running shoes for flat feet you can buy right now, including a wide range of options for walking, speedwork, trail running, and more.

Shopping for a daily trainer → Brooks Adrenaline GTS 25 (Men’s/Women’s)On a budget → New Balance Ellipse (Men’s/Women’s)Searching for some stability → Hoka Arahi 9 (Men’s/Women’s)Adding speedwork to your training → Brooks Ghost Amp (Men’s/Women’s)In need of more cushioned support → Saucony Guide 19 (Men’s/Women’s)Curious about zero-drop → Altra Fwd Via 2 (Men’s/Women’s)Training to race → New Balance SuperComp Rebel (Men’s/Women’s)Racing a marathon → Hoka Rocket X 3 (unisex)Pavement-averse → Hoka Speedgoat 7 (Men’s/Women’s)

Runner’s World Running Reviews Editor Amanda Furrer—who has relatively low arches—researched shoe testing data from our team of approximately 300 wear-testers. Our program includes runners of various experience levels, paces, gaits, and, most important for this guide, arch types. They logged hundreds of miles in these shoes, evaluating comfort, fit, and ride.

Our contributors also gathered intel from industry experts. Caitlin Giddings consulted Kimberly Davis, DC, ART, who is CEO and founder of RunLab in Austin, Texas, and physical therapist Jay Dicharry, author of Anatomy for Runners. Both Davis and Dicharry have extensive experience assessing and treating runners of all experience levels, gait types, and footwear needs; they were able to provide significant insight on the topic. Michael Charboneau, former RW editor and freelance reviewer for InsideHook, Men’s Journal, and other publications, also offered his expertise in our Q&A section.

Shop the best flat-footed running shoe deals on Amazon

The Brooks Adrenaline GTS 25 is as much a runner-fave as its neutral counterpart, the Brooks Ghost 18. This updated version of the Adrenaline GTS features even more cushioning. Brooks heightened the forefoot 3 mm and the heel 1 mm, bringing the offset down from 12 mm to 10 mm. This means more of a balanced feel underfoot. The nitrogen-infused DNA Loft v3 foam provides a responsive, plush ride for everyday training and long runs.

Tester Peter Vuong, an overpronator with a flat arch, gave his seal of approval.

“This is a solid, all-around shoe with good flexibility, comfort, and a smooth ride,” he said. “I loved my runs in this shoe. It’s comfortable and laces up nice and snug. The fit is near-perfect. I tested it mostly on the road with a little trail. I would use this shoe for many occasions including a 5K race or nice Sunday 10-miler. It’s nice to have something for multiple uses like that.”

The shoe has the brand’s holistic stability guide rails system. Dense foam on the medial and lateral sides of the Adrenaline GTS act as “bumpers” to help align the knee and ankle when form gets erratic from overpronating. Testers said the fit and support were spot-on.

“I’ve run in just about every model of this shoe, many of them predating the guide rails system,” said tester Chris Garges. “This shoe provides the stability that I need as a heel-striking overpronator. I had no issues with the stability even later into a 14-mile training run. Some prior Adrenalines and the Glycerin GTS shoes seem to have a touch too much foot movement, particularly in warm, sweaty weather, which causes blisters or loss of toenails. I did not experience those issues with these shoes.”

Shop Men’s at brooksrunning.com | Shop Women’s at brooksrunning.com

Think of the new Ellipse as your run club shoe; New Balance’s newest trainer is meant to make running easier with plenty of pop. The midsole is the brand’s Fresh Foam X, which ensures high energy return on your run as well as plenty of high-cushioned support that absorbs shock. It’s also a generous bed of plush for flat feet.

Testers praised the shoe’s cushioning, even though the ride for some felt slightly bulky. It’s not necessarily a speed shoe, but that didn’t stop some of our testers from going fast on that runner’s high. “It truly feels like I am at an advantage wearing these shoes,” said tester Laura Parks.

“The shoe is SO springy,” she continued. “The best word to describe the cushion is plush. I can feel my foot sinking into the cushion as it molds around me, while simultaneously still feeling the ground. The Ellipse has a cushy bounce sensation when I run in them that I haven’t quite felt before in other New Balance Fresh Foam models.”

Shop Men’s at newbalance.com | Shop Women’s at newbalance.com

We loved the Arahi 8 so much we bestowed it Gear of the Year. The Arahi 9 preserved all we loved with a few key updates, making the shoe even better. The dual-density compression-molded EVA midsole is retooled so the ride is even softer and more supportive. Think of the new midsole as a plush bed of comfort for your flat feet.

Unchanged is the stability tech. The trainer formerly had the brand’s J-Frame, which was a dense piece of J-shaped foam that wrapped around the heel. The H-Frame, a dense H-shaped piece of foam used in Hoka’s beefier shoes, like the Gaviota, provides a more balanced ride without overcorrecting your gait.

The Arahi 9 kept the previous iteration’s streamlined design. The Hoka team tweaked the fit, making the upper provide more medial support. We like lacing up in this versatile trainer for everyday runs, fartlek workouts, and recovery days.

Shop Men’s at hoka.com | Shop Women’s at hoka.com

The Ghost Amp is a game changer for the Ghost franchise because you truly get the best of both worlds: the signature comfort Brooks is known for (and why we always recommend the Ghost to beginner runners) and propulsive PEBA foam to spice up your runs.

Unlike the Ghost 18, which has a narrower fit than previous iterations, the Ghost Amp has ample toe room in the toebox and an accommodating fit in the midfoot area.

We recommend the Ghost Amp for speedwork but our testers found the shoe works for all kinds of runs.

“I try to save these shoes for my speed days, but I can’t stop lacing them for other runs as well,” said editor and tester Monique LeBrun, who has flat feet. “When you’re a slower runner, like me, you don’t need a super fancy plated shoes to pick up the pace, lightweight well-cushioned shoes will do just fine. Also, the PEBA/EVA blend is soft and bouncy, and that helps me pick up the pace, but it also helps keep momentum on slower days or if I stop to walk.”

Shop Men’s at brooksrunning.com | Shop Women’s at brooksrunning.com

Like many stability shoes these days, the Guide 19 provides support when you need it. The shoe has Saucony’s CenterPath technology, which uses higher sidewalls and a broader base to help stabilize erratic foot (and ankle and knee) movement. It also provides a locked-in, comfy fit that our testers favored for daily training and recovery runs.

The shoe has a slight rocker profile, which helps promote smooth heel-toe transitions on runs. Though the ride isn’t as responsive as other daily trainers (the Guide has Pwrrun foam, a proprietary EVA blend), it delivers a cushioned, yet supportive ride for collecting weekly mileage when you don’t feel like pushing the pace.

“Like its predecessor, this shoe made me feel good before even starting my runs,” said tester Dave Block. “I just love the comfort and feel! The shoe is very soft and slips on easily. I can’t think of any other shoe that feels this great, with the possible exception of the Adidas Pureboost, but that’s going back several years.”

Shop Men’s at saucony.com | Shop Women’s at saucony.com

If you thought choosing to run in a low-drop shoe meant a non-supportive barefoot ride (not the ideal for runners with flat feet), then you’ve never run in the Altra Fwd Via 2. Formerly known for making exclusively zero-drop trainers, Altra has released low-drop shoes these past couple of years, welcoming the zero-drop curious into their fold.

Shoes like the Fwd Via 2 have a 4 mm offset, which is merely the height of two stacked nickels. This low heel-to-toe drop helps runners ease into running in this type of trainer without fully making the plunge to zero drop. The plush cushioning also helps with the low-drop transition. Your arches won’t feel sore due to lack of support thanks to the thick bed of Altra Ego P35 midsole foam, which reaches 37 mm in the heel.

Like all of Altra’s shoes, a wide toebox provides wiggle room. The shoe also has a slight rocker shape to promote propulsion.

Shop Men’s at altrarunning.com | Shop Women’s at altrarunning.com

Think of the SuperComp Rebel as the successor to the SuperComp Elite v4, New Balance’s premium racing shoe beloved for its accessible features: a broader base, beefier cushioning for marathoners clocking over five hours, a rocker sole and carbon plate that’s propulsive but not too aggressive. The brand made the SuperComp Elite v6 snappier; it’s comparable to the Brooks Hyperion Elite 6. Though the SC Rebel is a “training companion” for longer efforts and carbon-plate conditioning, it’s also race day-worthy.

The midsole is the brand’s Infinion foam, which was featured in the max-cushioned 1080v15. The Infinion foam in the SC Rebel, however, is fine-tuned for springier, faster efforts.

“I enjoyed the energetic bounce I got running in these sneakers,” said tester Nina Rems. “My legs felt light and my feet felt quick. You definitely feel the difference running in sneakers with a carbon plate versus just foam soles. I was more responsive in these New Balance SuperComp Rebels than my regular trainers, New Balance 1080s.”

Shop Men’s at newbalance.com | Shop Women’s at newbalance.com

The Rocket X 3 is Hoka-soft with all the super shoe features. The broader base makes this shoe’s fit and ride not as severe as competitors’. Our midfoot didn’t feel squeezed, our toes weren’t crowded, and corner turns didn’t cause our life to flash before our eyes. What you get running in the Rocket X 3 is a stable—but fast—racing experience.

The shoe has dual-density PEBA foam and a winged carbon-fiber plate. Even though its ride is not as aggressive as the brand’s other racing shoe, the Cielo X1 2.0, it still packs a bouncy punch for running half marathons and marathons, and offers plenty of cushioning so your body won’t take a beating hours on the road.

“I love the push-off,” said Erica Bobstein. “They are bouncy and springy and they allow you to propel yourself forward with less effort. They do not have the same speedroll effect as the Saucony Endorphin Pro, but rather have an explosive push-off that requires less energy.”

A combination of plush maximum cushioning and all the tech you need to stay upright on the trail is a winning recipe for flat-footed runners tired of staying on the roads. The Speedgoat gets a reworking with its seventh iteration, resulting in a more secure—albeit, snug for wide feet—fit, and midsole foam that provides comfort for ultra distances.

Toothy lugs provide grip and stability over mixed, technical terrain, while the Meta-Rocker geometry smooths out heel-to-toe transitions. The supercritical EVA foam is tweaked to add more pop on the trail.

Tester Diane Grim, who previously tested the Speedgoat 6, noticed a significant difference in the shoe’s bounce with the 7. She found the ride more forgiving, “which could be great for the long runs, less foot fatigue.”

Shop Men’s at hoka.com | Shop Women’s at hoka.com

Some runners have anatomically flat feet, and other runners have what’s known as “collapsed arches,” which are flat because of a muscle weakness. Although the two types can look very similar, how you approach buying shoes for them varies widely, says Dr. Kimberly Davis of the RunLab, an Austin, Texas-based clinic that assesses running biomechanics and offers physical therapy and training.

Dr. Davis says that when it comes to shopping for a shoe for a flat-footed runner with collapsed arches due to muscle weakness, you can add arch support until the foot gets stronger and can support its own arch. But with an anatomically flat foot, arch support just sends stress up into the knee where it can lead to knee problems.

That’s why it’s important to know what type of flat foot you have before you settle on a shoe—and take into account not just your foot but your entire body, including knees, hips, and range of motion.

Added Arch Support Is Not Always the Answer

Runners with flat feet tend to overpronate, which is when the arches of the foot roll inward after landing. (This isn’t true across the board—there are plenty of flat-footed runners who are biomechanically sound and efficient, and don’t experience any overpronation.)

Up until recently, the running industry steered overpronators toward stability shoes to control this motion. Gradually, all that is starting to change with the realization that stability features don’t do much to correct the natural cycle of the foot, though some runners prefer having them.

Dr. Davis says people with flat feet often have really flexible feet that never get rigid for the push-off. “The footwear industry tries to solve that by putting an arch support in there to give them an arch or create suppination in the foot,” she says. “But that foot is structurally built that way, it’s not something you can solve with a shoe.”

Flat-Footed Runners Can Benefit From a Full-Contact Midsole

Jay Dicharry, author of Anatomy for Runners and director of the REP Lab in Bend, Oregon, agrees arch support can be detrimental because the arch is by nature dynamic, and having extra structure there can stop your foot from moving. Dicharry says flat-footed runners should put more focus on seeking out a shoe with a straight “last,” which is the mold that dictates the shape of the shoe. A straight-lasted shoe has a wider midfoot base and less of a cut-in, a profile that has fallen by the wayside in favor of hourglass-shaped shoes.

Most current shoes don’t provide much of a solid support surface for flat-footed runners, he says. “The problem is all these hourglass shoe shapes look nice on the wall, but when someone with a flat foot puts weight on one, part of their foot is bearing weight on the fabric upper,” he says. “The upper doesn’t work as a midsole for foot support. Feet do well when they’re on an even surface.”

The truth is most running shoes will work for most runners; however, if the shoes you’re using aren’t comfortable immediately or if you’re experiencing any pain while running, you should try a different pair. Get your movement pattern analyzed, whether at a clinic like the RunLab or even a running store that offers gait analysis.

Once you have more information about your feet and movement patterns, you can provide all that information to a running store to find the best shoe for you. Don’t be afraid to take a shoe out for a test run following assessment before purchasing anything.

Here’s a quick way to find out what kind of arches you have: Grab a piece of cardboard and head to the bathroom. Put the cardboard on the floor, wet the bottom of your feet with water in the bathtub or shower, then stand on the cardboard. If you see dry spaces where your arches are, you don’t have flat feet; if you see full footprints showing both soles, then your feet are flat.

As we noted in our explainer, there are multiple kinds of flat feet, and some feet are more flat than others. For a clear, detailed understanding of your foot shape and what kind of foot support you need, you should speak to a podiatrist or running expert.

How can I tell if I have naturally flat feet or collapsed arches?

People with naturally flat feet generally have the condition their entire lives. (Everyone is born with flat feet. Your arches develop in the first few years of life.) Collapsed arches are caused by muscle weakness or an injury. According to the Cleveland Clinic, the most common cause is “inflammation or a tear in the leg tendon (posterior tibial tendon) that supports the arch.”

Both naturally flat feet and collapsed arches can be painful, however, so if you’re experiencing discomfort, it’s best to consult with a doctor to determine which type you have.

My flat feet are causing pain and discomfort. What can I do to alleviate that?

Experts recommend stretching your Achilles, calves, and hamstrings. Stretching those tendons and muscles will help reduce strain on your feet. See our guides to strengthening and stretching the Achilles, calves, and hamstrings for specific exercises.

Amanda Furrer, Editor, Running Reviews, studied journalism at NYU and writing at Emerson College. She has reviewed gear and covered other topics in the running space for almost 10 years. Since 2013, she has consecutively run the Boston Marathon. She also has a master’s degree in gastronomy from Boston University and was formerly a professional baker for two years before hanging up her apron.

Michael is a freelance writer with years of experience covering gear and the outdoors for Runner's World and other publications; when he's not writing, he's usually biking, hiking, and running in the mountains around Los Angeles, where he lives.
